\displaystyle=y\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{C_{q}}\sqrt{j}\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot x\cdot h_{j-1}(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{C_{q}}\sqrt{j}\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot x\cdot h_{j-1}\big(x+y\sigma^{\prime}(x)\big)\bigg]

(C.12)

For the first summation in (
C.12
), only the
j=2
summand is nonzero, so we obtain

\displaystyle y\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{C_{q}}\sqrt{j}\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot x\cdot h_{j-1}(x)\bigg]=\sqrt{2}\alpha_{2}\cdot y.

(C.13)

For the second summation in (
C.12
), we have the following expansion:

\displaystyle y\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{C_{q}}j\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot x\cdot h_{j-1}\big(x+y\sigma^{\prime}(x)\big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad=y\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{C_{q}}j\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot x\cdot\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}r_{j-1,k}\cdot h_{j-k-1}(x)\cdot\big(y\sigma^{\prime}(x)\big)^{k}\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{k=0}^{C_{q}-1}\underbrace{\left\{\sum_{j=k+1}^{C_{q}}j\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot r_{j-1,k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[x\cdot h_{j-k-1}(x)\cdot\big(\sigma^{\prime}(x)\big)^{k}\right]\right\}}_{\displaystyle{:=\varsigma_{k}(\alpha)}}\cdot y^{k+1}.

(C.14)

where
\varsigma_{0}(\alpha),\ldots,\varsigma_{C_{q}-1}(\alpha)
are just polynomials of
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{C_{q}})
(recall the definition of
\sigma^{\prime}(x)
in (
C.3
)) and each
r_{j-1,k}
is a positive number.
Combining (
C.13
) and (
C.14
), we get the following decomposition of
\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)=\sqrt{2}\alpha_{2}\cdot y+\sum_{k=0}^{C_{q}-1}\varsigma_{k}(\alpha)\cdot y^{k+1}.

(C.15)

Further using
y=p(x)
and the definition of
\zeta_{2}(y)
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{2}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[h_{2}(x)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)]

(C.16)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\alpha_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[h_{2}(x)\cdot p(x)\right]+\sum_{k=0}^{C_{q}-1}\varsigma_{k}(\alpha)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[h_{2}(x)\cdot p(x)^{k+1}\right].

(C.17)

According to Proposition 5 of
39
, we can set
C_{q}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
(only depending on
q
) such that there exists a smallest
I\leq C_{q}
such that the information exponent
q^{\star}(p^{I})\leq 2
.
We notice that in this case
s^{\star}(p)=2
, where we abuse notation and let
s^{\star}(p)
be the generative exponent of the polynomial
p
.
In fact,
s^{\star}(p)=1
means
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}}[{\mathcal{T}}(y)\cdot h_{1}(x)]\equiv 0
for all label transformations
{\mathcal{T}}
.
Hence, the only possibility is that
q^{\star}(p^{I})=2
since
p^{I}
is just a special case of a label transformation and we cannot get any first-order term from
p^{I}
.
Therefore, we further simplify the target quantity as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s^{\star}-1}(y)]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\alpha_{2}\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[h_{2}(x)\cdot p(x)\right]}_{\displaystyle{\defeq b_{1}}}+\sum_{k=I}^{C_{q}}\varsigma_{k-1}(\alpha)\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[h_{2}(x)\cdot p(x)^{k}\right]}_{\displaystyle\defeq b_{k}}

(C.18)

\displaystyle=b_{1}\cdot\sqrt{2}\alpha_{2}+\sum_{k=I}^{C_{q}}b_{k}\cdot\varsigma_{k-1}(\alpha),

(C.19)

where
b_{I}\neq 0
according to the definition of
I
.
Now we take a closer look at the polynomials
\{\varsigma_{k}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{C_{q}})\}_{k=I-1}^{C_{q}-1}
.
We claim that: (i) they are different polynomials and are linearly independent, and (ii) in particular, they are all nonzero.
To see these, recall that

\displaystyle\varsigma_{k}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{C_{q}})=\sum_{j=k+1}^{C_{q}}\sqrt{j}\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot r_{j-1,k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[x\cdot h_{j-k-1}(x)\cdot\big(\sigma^{\prime}(x)\big)^{k}\right],

(C.20)

where
\sigma^{\prime}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{C_{q}}\sqrt{j}\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot h_{j-1}(x)
.
We can calculate that for
k=0
,
\varsigma_{0}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{C_{q}})=\sqrt{2}\alpha_{2}
, which is a nonzero polynomial.
For
k=1
, we have that

\displaystyle\varsigma_{1}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{C_{q}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{C_{q}}\sqrt{j}\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot r_{j-1,1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[x\cdot h_{j-2}(x)\cdot\sigma^{\prime}(x)\right]

(C.21)

\displaystyle=2r_{1,1}\cdot\alpha_{2}^{2}+\sum_{j=3}^{C_{q}}\sqrt{j}\cdot\alpha_{j}\cdot r_{j-1,1}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[x\cdot h_{j-2}(x)\cdot\sigma^{\prime}(x)\right]

(C.22)

which is nonzero (since in the summation from
j=3
to
C_{q}
there would be no term in the form of
\alpha_{2}^{2}
) and is linearly independent of
\varsigma_{0}
because each term in the summation here has degree exactly
2
.
Now consider for
k\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\varsigma_{k}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{C_{q}})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2(k+1)}k\cdot r_{k,k}\cdot\alpha_{1}^{k-1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{k+1}

(C.23)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=k+2}^{C_{q}}\sqrt{j}\alpha_{j}r_{j-1,k}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{x\sim{\mathcal{N}}}\left[x\cdot h_{j-k-1}(x)\cdot\big(\sigma^{\prime}(x)\big)^{k}\right].

(C.24)

Again, this polynomial is nonzero (since in the summation from
j=k+2
to
C_{q}
there would be no term in the form of
\alpha_{1}^{k-1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{k+1}
) and is linearly independent of
\varsigma_{0},\cdots,\varsigma_{k-1}
because the highest degree of these polynomials is no larger than
k
, and the degree of each term in
\varsigma_{k}
is exactly
k+1
.
Thus we have proved the two claims by induction.
Now recall that we are aiming to prove that the RHS of (
C.19
) is nonzero.
By our two claims just proved, the RHS of (
C.19
) is a linear combination of
C_{q}-I+2
linearly independent and nonzero polynomials where at least one of the combination coefficients is nonzero (which is
b_{I}
).
Thus we obtain that the RHS of (
C.19
) is a nonzero polynomial of
(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{C_{q}})
and its zeros form a measure-zero set.
This proves that with probability
1
over the randomness of
(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{C_{q}})
, the high-pass condition holds.

Case 2:
s^{\star}=1
.
For this case of
s^{\star}=1
, the proof is almost the same as that for
s^{\star}=2
, where we additionally utilize the fact that a polynomial link function with generative exponent
s^{\star}=1
cannot be an even polynomial (
Example

2.2
) and thus there always exists some
I\leq C_{q}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
such that the information exponent
q^{\star}(p^{I})=1
(see Proposition 5 of
39
). With this fact, repeating the above argument gives the desired high-pass property.

Verifying the event
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Now we verify that the desired event

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}=\big\{|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}|\leq d^{-10s^{\star}},\forall(m,l,i,t)\in[M]\times[L]\times[n]\times[T]\big\}

(C.25)

holds with probability at least
1-O(d^{-c_{0}})
for some constant
c_{0}>0
that we specify later.
With (
C.6
) and (
C.10
), it suffices to look at the error terms
\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}
,
\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,2}^{(t)}
, and
\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,3}^{(t)}
separately.
For
\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}\right|\leq\sum_{m^{\prime}=1}^{M}|a_{m^{\prime}}|\cdot\left|\sigma(\langle w_{m^{\prime},l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle)\right|\cdot\left|\sigma^{\prime}(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle)\right|

(C.26)

Note that
\{\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle\}_{m\in[M]}
are standard Gaussians since
\{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m\in[M]}\subset\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
Therefore, with probability at least
1-d^{-c_{0}}
for some constant
c_{0}>0
, we have that
|\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(1)
.
Meanwhile, since
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are both polynomials with constant order and bounded coefficients, and since
M=O(d)
, by taking
a_{m}=d^{-12s^{\star}}
, we conclude from (
C.26
) that

\displaystyle\big|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}\big|\leq\widetilde{O}(d^{-10s^{\star}}).

(C.27)

For the second error term
\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,2}^{(t)}
, similarly we have that

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,2}^{(t)}\right|\leq\sum_{m^{\prime}\in[M]}|a_{m^{\prime}}|\cdot\left|\sigma(\langle(w^{(t)}_{m^{\prime},l})^{+},z_{i}\rangle)\right|\cdot\left|\sigma^{\prime}(\langle(w^{(t)}_{m,l})^{+},z_{i}\rangle)\right|.

(C.28)

Note that the one-step updated weights satisfy

\displaystyle\left|\langle(w^{(t)}_{m,l})^{+},z_{i}\rangle\right|=\left|\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle+y_{i}\cdot\sigma^{\prime}(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle)+\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}\right|=\widetilde{O}(1),

(C.29)

with probability at least
1-d^{-c_{0}}
since
y_{i}=p(\langle\theta^{\star},z_{i}\rangle)
and
p
is also a polynomial of constant degree and coefficients.
Therefore, with the choices of
a_{m}
, by (
C.28
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,2}^{(t)}\right|\leq\widetilde{O}(d^{-10s^{\star}}).

(C.30)

Finally, regarding
\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,3}^{(t)}
, note that with the same argument as (
C.29
), we know that with probability at least
1-d^{-c_{0}}
, both
\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle+y_{i}\cdot\sigma^{\prime}(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle)+\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}
and
\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle+y_{i}\cdot\sigma^{\prime}(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle)
are
\widetilde{O}(1)
.
Since
\sigma^{\prime}
is a polynomial, it is
\widetilde{O}(1)
-Lipschitz continuous for inputs that are
\widetilde{O}(1)
.
Therefore, combining this with (
C.27
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\big|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,3}^{(t)}\big|\leq|y_{i}|\cdot\widetilde{O}\big(|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i,1}^{(t)}|\big)=\widetilde{O}(d^{-10s^{\star}}).

(C.31)

Finally, combining (
C.27
), (
C.30
), and (
C.31
), we obtain that for a given
(m,l,i,t)
, with probability at least
1-O(d^{-c_{0}})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\big|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}\big|\leq d^{-10s^{\star}}

(C.32)

for all sufficiently large
d
.
Under the parameter choices in
Theorem

4.2
,
MLnT=\widetilde{\Theta}(d^{(s^{\star}+1)/2+(s^{\star}/2\vee 1)})
.
Finally, taking
c_{0}
larger than
(s^{\star}+1)/2+(s^{\star}/2\vee 1)
and applying a union bound, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}({\mathcal{E}})\geq 1-MLnT\cdot\widetilde{O}(d^{-c_{0}})\geq 1-\widetilde{\Theta}\left(d^{(s^{\star}+1)/2+(s^{\star}/2\vee 1)}\right)\cdot\widetilde{O}(d^{-c_{0}})\geq 1-\widetilde{O}(d^{-c_{0}^{\prime}})

(C.33)

for some other constant
c_{0}^{\prime}>0
.
Here we have applied our choice of
(M,L,n,T)
in
Theorem

4.2
.
Thus we verify the property of the event
{\mathcal{E}}
, proving Corollary
4.5
.
∎

C.2.2
Modified loss for general
s^{\star}\geq 1

Here we give a specific choice of the activation function
\sigma
and the loss function
\ell
.
We mainly focus on the situation where
\mathbb{Q}_{y}
has a continuous cumulative distribution function
F_{\mathbb{Q}_{y}}
with bounded density
f_{\mathbb{Q}_{y}}
.
For the situation where
\mathbb{Q}_{y}
is a discrete distribution (e.g., classification tasks), we discuss it at the end of this section.
For the activation function
\sigma
, we let
\sigma(x):=(1/\sqrt{s^{\star}})\cdot h_{s^{\star}}(x)
.
Since then,

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{s}(y)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\psi(y,x)\cdot h_{s}(x){\,|\,}y]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\sigma^{\prime}(x)\cdot h_{s}(x)]\cdot\ell^{\prime}(y)=0,\quad\forall s<s^{\star}-1.

(C.34)

Regarding the choice of the loss function
\ell
, we remark that if one chooses a fixed loss function, there always exist instances such that the second assumption in the high-pass condition fails.
To address this issue, we propose to construct a random loss function
\ell
.
To rule out pathological examples of the underlying distribution
\mathbb{P}
, we make the following assumption on the coefficient function
\zeta_{s^{\star}}
.

Assumption C.2
.

We assume that the expansion of
\widetilde{\zeta}_{s^{\star}}:=\zeta_{s^{\star}}\circ F_{\mathbb{Q}_{y}}^{-1}:[0,1]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
on the Fourier basis
\{\varphi_{i}(x)\}_{i\geq 0}
of
[0,1]
has a non-zero coefficient of order at most
D=O(1)
.

We then choose the loss function
\ell
as the following,

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}(y)=\sum_{i=0}^{D}\alpha_{i}\cdot\varphi_{i}\circ F_{\mathbb{Q}_{y}}(y),\quad\alpha_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}([0,1]),\quad\forall 0\leq i\leq D.

(C.35)

Notice that
F_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{y}}
can be estimated from data using a one-dimensional density estimator.
Thus here we directly assume the accessibility of the function
F_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{y}}
.
This further gives that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s^{\star}-1}(y)]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\ell^{\prime}(y)]=\sum_{i=0}^{D}\alpha_{i}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{Unif}([0,1])}\left[\widetilde{\zeta}_{s^{\star}}(\widetilde{y})\cdot\varphi_{i}(\widetilde{y})\right],

(C.36)

which is a non-zero polynomial of the coefficients
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\leq D}
due to
C.2
.

Proof of Corollary
4.7
.

To prove
Corollary

4.7
, it suffices to show that (i)
4.1
holds, and (ii) the event
{\mathcal{E}}
holds with the desired high probability.
In the following, we first verify
4.1
, and then check the event
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Verifying
4.1
.

First, since
\sigma^{\prime}
is a polynomial and
\ell^{\prime}
is bounded (the Fourier basis is bounded and
D=O(1)
), we know that both
(a)
and
(c)
are satisfied.
Then, by the discussion before the proof, we know from (
C.34
) that the first condition in the high-pass assumption (
(b)
) is satisfied.
Furthermore, according to (
C.36
) and
C.2
,
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s^{\star}-1}(y)]
is a nonzero polynomial of the coefficients
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\leq D}
and thus its zeros form a measure-zero set.
This means that with probability
1
over the randomness of
(\alpha_{0},\cdots,\alpha_{D})
, the second condition in the high-pass assumption is also satisfied. This verifies
4.1
.

Verifying the event
{\mathcal{E}}
.

Recall our definition in
Example

4.6
, the error term is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}=\left(\ell^{\prime}(y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}\left(y_{i}-f(z_{i};\{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m\in[M]}\})\right)\right)\cdot\sigma^{\prime}(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle).

(C.37)

First, since
w_{m,l}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle
is a standard Gaussian and therefore
|\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle|=\widetilde{O}(1)
with probability at least
1-d^{-c_{0}}
for some constant
c_{0}>0
.
Since
\sigma^{\prime}
is a polynomial of constant degree, we then obtain that
|\sigma^{\prime}(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle)|=\widetilde{O}(1)
with probability at least
1-d^{-c_{0}}
.
Second, consider that the second derivative of the loss function
\ell^{\prime\prime}(y)
is given by

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime\prime}(y)=\sum_{i=0}^{D}\alpha_{i}\cdot\varphi_{i}^{\prime}\left(F_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{y}}(y)\right)\cdot f_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{y}}(y),

(C.38)

which satisfies
|\ell^{\prime\prime}(y)|=O(1)
since the derivative of the Fourier basis is still bounded and the density of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{y}
is assumed to be bounded.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}(y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}\left(y_{i}-f(z_{i};\{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m\in[M]}\})\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\left|f(z_{i};\{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m\in[M]}\})\right|\right)

(C.39)

\displaystyle=O\left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}|a_{m}|\cdot\left|\sigma(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle)\right|\right).

(C.40)

Since
\sigma
is a polynomial of constant degree and
\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle
are all standard Gaussians, we have that
|\sigma(\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}\rangle)|=\widetilde{O}(1)
with probability at least
1-O(d^{-c_{0}})
.
Now given that
M=O(d)
and taking
a_{m}=d^{-12s^{\star}}
, we have that

\displaystyle\ell^{\prime}(y_{i})-\ell^{\prime}\left(y_{i}-f(z_{i};\{w_{m,l}^{(t)}\}_{m\in[M]}\})\right)

\displaystyle=O(d^{-10s^{\star}}),

(C.41)

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-c_{0}})
.
Therefore, for any given
(m,l,i,t)
, with probability at least
1-O(d^{-c_{0}})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\big|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}\big|\leq d^{-10s^{\star}}

(C.42)

for all sufficiently large
d
.
Finally, as in the proof of
Corollary

4.5
, we take
c_{0}
larger than
(s^{\star}+1)/2+(s^{\star}/2\vee 1)
and apply a union bound to obtain that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}({\mathcal{E}})\geq 1-MLnT\cdot\widetilde{O}(d^{-c_{0}})\geq 1-\widetilde{\Theta}\left(d^{(s^{\star}+1)/2+(s^{\star}/2\vee 1)}\right)\cdot\widetilde{O}(d^{-c_{0}})\geq 1-\widetilde{O}(d^{-c_{0}^{\prime}})

(C.43)

for some other constant
c_{0}^{\prime}>0
.
Thus we verify the property of the event
{\mathcal{E}}
, proving Corollary
4.7
.
∎

Remark C.3

(Discrete labels)
.

For the case of a discrete label
y
supported on a finite set
{\mathcal{Y}}
(e.g., classification tasks), the construction of
\psi
is more direct.
In this case, we can still consider an oracle function in the form of
\psi(y,x)=\sigma^{\prime}(x)\cdot\varphi(y)
for some function
\varphi(y)
.
The activation function
\sigma(x)=(1/\sqrt{s^{\star}})\cdot h_{s^{\star}}(x)
and the function
\varphi(y)
are given by

\displaystyle\varphi(y)\sim\mathrm{Unif}([0,1]),\quad\forall y\in{\mathcal{Y}}.

(C.44)

On the one hand, we can directly conclude as in (
C.34
) that
\widehat{\psi}_{s}(y)=0
for all
s<s^{\star}-1
.
On the other hand, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s^{\star}-1}(y)]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\varphi(y)]=\sum_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{y}(y)\cdot\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\varphi(y).

(C.45)

By the definition of generative exponent (
Definition

2.1
),
\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{y}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)^{2}]>0
and thus at least one of
\{{\mathbb{Q}}_{y}(y)\cdot\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\}_{y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}
is non-zero.
Thus under (
C.44
),
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s^{\star}-1}(y)]
is non-zero with probability
1
over the randomness in
\varphi
.
Thus we have verified
(b)
.

(a)
and
(c)
can be verified in the same way as for the continuous case, and thus
4.1
is checked.
Finally, we remark that in the discrete case we do not attempt to derive the oracle function from a loss derivative and thus simply set the error terms
\mathrm{err}
as zero.
Thus all the conditions in Theorem
4.2
hold and Corollary
4.7
is proved.

Appendix D
Proof of the Main Theorem for the Uniform Prior

Now we present the proof for
Theorem

4.2
.
For convenience, we introduce the following shorthand.

Definition D.1

(Shorthand for the alignment)
.

For
\theta\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
, we denote
\rho=\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\rangle
as the alignment between
\theta
and
\theta^{\star}
, which also inherits the subscript and superscript of
\theta
as well, i.e.,
\rho_{m}^{(t)}=\langle\theta_{m}^{(t)},\theta^{\star}\rangle
.

Recall from
Algorithm

1
that in each step, given the normalized gradient step
{\widebar{g}}_{m}^{(t)}=g_{m}^{(t)}/\|g_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}
, the updated weight parameter is given by

\displaystyle\theta_{m}^{(t+1)}=\frac{\theta_{m}^{(t)}+\eta{\widebar{g}}_{m}^{(t)}}{\big\|\theta_{m}^{(t)}+\eta{\widebar{g}}_{m}^{(t)}\big\|_{2}}.

(D.1)

It is clear that the alignment
\rho_{m}^{(t+1)}=\langle\theta_{m}^{(t+1)},\theta^{\star}\rangle
is determined jointly by the alignment
\rho_{m}^{(t)}
of the previous iterate and the alignment
\langle{\widebar{g}}_{m}^{(t)},\theta^{\star}\rangle
of the normalized gradient in the current step.
Therefore, we will first analyze the properties of the normalized gradient
{\widebar{g}}_{m}^{(t)}
in
Section

D.1
, and then use these properties to analyze the dynamics of
\rho_{m}^{(t)}
.

D.1
Properties of the Gradient Step

Recall from
Algorithm

1
that each gradient
g_{m,l,i}^{(t)}
contains an error part
\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}z_{i}^{(t)}
.
This error term can be made negligible by setting the weights of the second layer to be sufficiently small, and the moments of
{\widebar{g}}_{m}^{(t)}
are determined primarily by
\{\psi(y_{i}^{(t)},\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}^{(t)}\rangle)z_{i}^{(t)}\}
.
Therefore, we first analyze the properties of the normalized gradient
{\widebar{g}}_{m}^{(t)}
without the error part, while the error part will be taken into account in
Section

D.2
.
To this end, we introduce the following definition for the gradient without the error term.

Definition D.2
.

Under
4.1
, consider any fixed
\theta\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
and data
(z_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(z_{n},y_{n})\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}
.
For polarization level
\gamma\in(0,1/2)
, define
w_{l}=(\gamma\theta+\xi_{l})/\|\gamma\theta+\xi_{l}\|_{2}
for
l=1,\ldots,L
, where
\xi_{1},\ldots\xi_{L}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}{\text{Unif}}({\mathbb{S}}^{d-1})
are independent of
\{(z_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, and define the corresponding gradient as

\displaystyle{\widehat{g}}=\frac{1}{nL}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\big(\psi(y_{i},\langle w_{l},z_{i}\rangle)\cdot z_{i}-\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y_{i})\cdot w_{l}\big).

(D.2)

Moreover, let
v_{1}=\theta^{\star}
and
v_{2}=(\theta-\rho\theta^{\star})/\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}
, and further expand them to the following orthonormal basis for
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
:

\displaystyle v_{1}=\theta^{\star},v_{2}=\frac{\theta-\rho\theta^{\star}}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}},v_{3},\ldots,v_{d}.

(D.3)

Corresponding to the above orthonormal basis, define the following nice events for
\epsilon,\widetilde{\epsilon}>0
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\Big\{\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|\langle\xi_{l},v_{i}\rangle|<\epsilon,\quad\forall l\in[L]\Big\};

(D.4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\widetilde{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\left\{|\langle\xi_{l},\xi_{l^{\prime}}\rangle|<\widetilde{\epsilon},\quad\forall l,l^{\prime}\in[L]\quad\mathrm{s.t.}\quad l\neq l^{\prime}\right\}.

(D.5)

Perturbed weights: near uniformity and orthogonality

When
d
is large, the perturbation noise vectors
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{L}
are nearly orthogonal to each other and to any fixed direction with high probability.
Also, we will use a small polarization level
\gamma
(roughly
d^{-1/4}
), which implies that the perturbed weights
w_{1},\ldots,w_{L}
are nearly orthogonal as well, which is formalized in the lemma below.

Lemma D.3

(Perturbed weights on the nice event)
.

Under the setting introduced in
Definition

D.2
, on the nice event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
, it holds for all
l\in[L]
that

\displaystyle|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|\leq 2(\gamma|\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\rangle|+\epsilon),\quad|\langle w_{l},v_{2}\rangle|\leq 2\Big(\gamma\sqrt{1-\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2}}+\epsilon\Big),

\displaystyle|\langle w_{l},v_{i}\rangle|\leq 2\cdot\epsilon,\quad 3\leq i\leq d.

Additionally, on the nice event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\widetilde{\epsilon})
,
\langle{w_{l}},{w_{l^{\prime}}}\rangle\leq 4(\gamma^{2}+2\gamma\epsilon+\widetilde{\epsilon})
for any
l\neq l^{\prime}
.

Proof of
Lemma

D.3
.

See
Section

D.3.1
.
∎

Characterizing error-free gradient

Next, we characterize
\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle/\|{\widehat{g}}\|_{2}
in two steps:

1.

In
Proposition

D.4
, we analyze the expected magnitude of
\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle
and
P_{\theta^{\star}}^{\perp}{\widehat{g}}
under
{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}
.

2.

In
Proposition

D.5
, we establish the concentration of
{\widehat{g}}
along each direction.

Both propositions are established under the setting of
Definition

D.2
, and the proof of these propositions is deferred to
Section

D.3
.
A key ingredient in the proof is the following lemma for the decomposition of the first moment of the gradient (see
Appendix

G
for a proof).

Lemma

G.2

(Decomposition of the gradient)
.

Under the setting of
Definition

D.2
, it holds that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\geq s^{\star}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s-1}(y)]\cdot\frac{\sqrt{s}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s-1}\cdot\theta^{\star}

(D.6)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{s\geq s^{\star}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)]\cdot\frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s}\cdot w_{l}.

(D.7)

We see from the above expression that
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]
is a linear combination of
\theta^{\star}
and
w_{l}
.
Since
\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle
is small on the nice events by
Lemma

D.3
, the dominant term in the signal direction corresponds to the coefficient
\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}
.
This is indeed a consequence of the high-pass assumption in
(b)
and the recursive structure of the Hermite polynomials.
The following proposition provides a quantitative characterization for this.

Proposition D.4

(Alignment of expected gradient)
.

Under the setting of
Definition

D.2
, let
T=\Theta(\log d)
, and suppose that
1-\Pr({\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon))=O(d^{-s^{\star}/2})
for
\gamma=o(1)
and
\epsilon=o(1)
.
Set

\displaystyle L\gtrsim\Big(\big((\epsilon\vee\gamma)^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot d^{s^{\star}/2}\big)\vee d\Big)\cdot\log d.

(D.8)

Then for any constant
c>0
, there exists a
\{\xi_{l}\}_{l\in[L]}
-measurable event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}
with
\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{1})\geq 1-d^{-c}(MT)^{-1}
, such that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
, the following holds:

•

The dominant term in the alignment of
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]
satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{s^{\star}}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot{\widehat{\psi}}_{s^{\star}-1}(y)]\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}]\simeq\begin{cases}\gamma\rho\cdot(\gamma|\rho|+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-2}&\text{if }s^{\star}\text{ is even};\\
(\gamma|\rho|+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-1}&\text{if }s^{\star}\text{ is odd}.\end{cases}

(D.9)

•

The residual error can be bounded as

\displaystyle\big|\langle{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle-\sqrt{s^{\star}}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot{\widehat{\psi}}_{s^{\star}-1}(y)]\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}]\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim(\gamma|\rho|+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}.

(D.10)

•

Also,
|\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}-|\langle{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle||\lesssim(\gamma|\rho|+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}
.

Proof of
Proposition

D.4
.

See
Section

D.3.2
.
∎

Complementary to the above result on the expected gradient, the next proposition provides a concentration result for
{\widehat{g}}
.

Proposition D.5

(Concentration of the gradient)
.

Under the settings in
Definition

D.2
, suppose
\epsilon^{2}\leq\widetilde{\epsilon}\ll 1
and
2\gamma\epsilon\leq\widetilde{\epsilon}
.
Also, suppose that sample size
n
satisfies

\displaystyle n=\Omega\Big(\big((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1}\big)^{-1}\cdot\log(d)^{2C_{p}+2}\Big),

(D.11)

where
C_{p}
is the constant defined in
(c)
.
Then there exists a
\{(z_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
-measurable event
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
with
\Pr({{\mathcal{E}}_{2}})\geq 1-d^{-c}\cdot(MT)^{-1}
such that the following holds on
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}\cap{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\widetilde{\epsilon})
:

•

First,
\langle\widehat{g},\theta^{\star}\rangle
concentrates around its expectation as

\displaystyle\big|\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle-\langle{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle\big|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1})\cdot\log(d)}{n}}.

(D.12)

•

Second,
\widehat{g}
also concentrates around its expectation as

\displaystyle\big\|{\widehat{g}}-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\big\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1})\cdot d\log(d)}{n}}.

(D.13)

Proof of
Proposition

D.5
.

See
Section

D.3.3
.
∎

D.2
Proof of the Main Theorem for Uniform Prior

Now we are ready to present the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

To start with, we clarify the event we will work with by verifying that our configuration is compatible with the conditions in
Proposition

D.4
and
Proposition

D.5
. We denote

\displaystyle{\widehat{g}}_{m}^{(t)}=\frac{1}{nL}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\big(\psi(y_{i}^{(t)},\langle w_{m,l}^{(t)},z_{i}^{(t)}\rangle)\cdot z_{i}^{(t)}-\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y_{i}^{(t)})\cdot w_{m,l}^{(t)}\big).

(D.14)

Let
\theta_{m}^{(t)}
be the
m
-th neuron at the
t
-th step, and denote
\rho_{m}^{(t)}=\langle{\theta_{m}^{(t)}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle
. We choose the corresponding orthonormal basis as

\displaystyle v_{m,1}^{(t)}=\theta^{\star},v_{m,2}^{(t)}=\frac{\theta_{m}^{(t)}-\rho_{m}^{(t)}\theta^{\star}}{\sqrt{1-{\rho_{m}^{(t)}}^{2}}},v_{m,3}^{(t)},\ldots,v_{m,d}^{(t)}.

(D.15)

Also, we define the associated good events as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\big\{|\langle\xi_{m,l}^{(t)},\theta^{\star}\rangle|<\epsilon,\quad\max_{2\leq i\leq d}|\langle\xi_{m,l}^{(t)},v_{m,i}^{(t)}\rangle|<\epsilon,\quad\forall l\in[L]\big\};

(D.16)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}(\widetilde{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\big\{|\langle\xi_{m,l}^{(t)},\xi_{m,l^{\prime}}^{(t)}\rangle|<\widetilde{\epsilon},\quad\forall l,l^{\prime}\in[L]\quad\mathrm{s.t.}\quad l\neq l^{\prime}\big\}.

(D.17)

With these notations, we see that
{\widehat{g}}_{m}^{(t)}
, the mini-batch data
\{(y_{i}^{(t)},z_{i}^{(t)})\}_{i\in[n]}
, and the good events
{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}(\epsilon)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}({\widetilde{\epsilon}})
together form an instance in
Definition

D.2
.

Recall that
\gamma=(d^{-1}\cdot\log d)^{1/4}=o(1)
is fixed throughout the algorithm.
We set
\epsilon=d^{-1/2}\log d
.
Lemma

H.15
implies that for any
t
and
m
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr\Big({{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}(\epsilon)}^{c}\Big)\leq Ld\cdot\big(\exp(-d/16)+d^{-\log d/4}\big)

(D.18)

For the number of perturbations, we choose
L=\Omega\big(d^{(s^{\star}+1)/2}\log d\big)
, which is at most polynomial in
d
.
Therefore
\Pr\Big({{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}(\epsilon)}^{c}\Big)
decays faster than any constant-degree polynomial in
d
.
For sufficiently large
d
, it holds that
\Pr\Big({{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}(\epsilon)}^{c}\Big)=O(d^{-s^{\star}/2})
.
On the other hand, the choice of
L
ensures that

\displaystyle L\gtrsim\big(d^{(s^{\star}+1)/4}\cdot\log(d)^{s^{\star}-1})\vee d\cdot\log d\simeq\big((\epsilon\vee\gamma)^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot d^{s^{\star}/2}\vee d\big)\cdot\log d.

(D.19)

So far, we see that all the conditions in
Proposition

D.4
are satisfied and we denote the associated event therein as
{\mathcal{E}}_{m,1}^{(t)}
.

Next, we verify the conditions in
Proposition

D.5
. We choose
\widetilde{\epsilon}=\sqrt{4\big(c+\log_{d}(MTL^{2})\big)\cdot d^{-1}\log d}
, then it holds by
Lemma

H.15
that

\displaystyle\Pr\Big(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}(\widetilde{\epsilon})^{c}\Big)\leq L^{2}\cdot\big(\exp(-d/16)+d^{-(c+\log_{d}(MTL^{2}))}\big)\lesssim d^{-c}/MT.

(D.20)

Additionally, we see that both
\epsilon^{2}\leq{\widetilde{\epsilon}}\ll 1
and
2\gamma\epsilon\leq{\widetilde{\epsilon}}
are satisfied for sufficiently large
d
.
It is easily verified that our choice of
L=\Omega\big(d^{(s^{\star}+1)/2}\vee(d\log d)\big)
clearly meets the condition that

\displaystyle L\gtrsim d^{(s^{\star}-1)/2}\gtrsim(\gamma^{2}+{\widetilde{\epsilon}})^{-(s^{\star}-1)}.

(D.21)

Therefore, plugging in
\gamma,{\widetilde{\epsilon}}
and the lower bound on
L
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\log(d)^{2C_{p}+2}}{\big((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1}\big)}\lesssim\log(d)^{2C_{p}+2}\cdot d^{(s^{\star}-1)/2}

(D.22)

Since we choose
n\gtrsim(d\log d)^{s^{\star}/2}\vee d\log d
, it follows from the last inequality that

\displaystyle n\gtrsim\frac{\log(d)^{2C_{p}+2}}{\big((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1}\big)}.

(D.23)

Hence, all the conditions in
Proposition

D.5
are satisfied and we denote the associated event therein as
{\mathcal{E}}_{m,2}^{(t)}
.

Noting that the gradient in
Definition

D.2
does not include the error term
\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}
, we need an upper bound on
\|z_{i}^{(t)}\|_{2}
for the purpose of controlling
\|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}\cdot z_{i}^{(t)}\|_{2}
. We define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{m,3}^{(t)}=\left\{\max_{i\in[n]}\|z_{i}^{(t)}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{d}\right\}.

(D.24)

By standard Bernstein’s inequality, we have that
\Pr\Big({{\mathcal{E}}_{m,3}^{(t)}}^{c}\Big)\leq Ld\cdot\exp\{-d/8\}=O(\exp\{-C^{\prime}d\})
for some
C^{\prime}>0
.
Thanks to the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{m,3}^{(t)}
, we always have for any
v\in{\mathbb{S}}^{d-1}
that

\displaystyle\big|\langle{g_{m}^{(t)}},{v}\rangle-\langle{{\widehat{g}}_{m}^{(t)}},{v}\rangle\big|\vee\big|\|g_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}-\|{\widehat{g}}_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\|g_{m}^{(t)}-{\widehat{g}}_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}

(D.25)

\displaystyle\leq d^{1/2}\cdot\max_{l,i}|\mathrm{err}_{m,l,i}^{(t)}|

(D.26)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-9s^{\star}}.

(D.27)

To put things together, we work on the following event:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}=\bigcap_{m=1}^{M}\bigcap_{t=1}^{T}\big({\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}(\epsilon)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{m}^{(t)}({\widetilde{\epsilon}})\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{m,1}^{(t)}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{m,2}^{(t)}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{m,3}^{(t)}\big),

(D.28)

which satisfies
\Pr({\mathcal{E}})\geq 1-O(d^{-c})
for some
c>0
by the union bound argument.

In the sequel, we restrict our attention to neurons that have at least
d^{-1/2}/2
alignment, i.e., the indices
m
such that
|\langle{\theta_{m}^{(0)}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|\geq d^{-1/2}/2
.
From now on, we drop the neuron index
m
and the iteration index
{(t)}
in the following analysis for simplicity. The updated weight parameter is denoted as
\theta^{\prime}
, and the alignment after the update is denoted as
\rho^{\prime}=\langle{\theta^{\prime}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle
.
By rotational invariance of the random initialization, there exists an absolute constant
p_{0}>0
such that for all sufficiently large
d
,

\displaystyle\Pr\big(|\langle{\theta_{m}^{(0)}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|\geq d^{-1/2}/2\big)\geq p_{0}.

(D.29)

Since
M=C_{M}\log d
with
C_{M}
sufficiently large, a Chernoff bound implies that with probability at least
1-O(d^{-c})
for some constant
c>0
, the number of neurons with initial alignment
|\rho|\geq d^{-1/2}/2
is at least
\Omega(M)
.
For convenience, define

\displaystyle\kappa\defeq\frac{n}{(d\log d)^{s^{\star}/2}\lor d\log d}\cdot(\log d)^{-1}=\Omega(1).

(D.30)

Recall that we defined
{\widehat{g}}
as the error-free gradient.
Under the preceding configuration,
Proposition

D.5
and Eq. (
D.27
) together imply that the error of
\langle g,\theta^{\star}\rangle
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\big|\langle{g},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle]\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1})\cdot\log(d)}{n}}+\big|\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle-\langle{g},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle\big|

(D.31)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{(d^{-1}\log d)^{(s^{\star}-1)/2}\cdot\log(d)}{n}}+d^{-9s^{\star}}

(D.32)

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}d^{-(2s^{\star}-1)/4}\cdot(\log d)^{-1/4}\cdot\kappa^{-1/2}&\text{if $s^{\star}\geq 2$},\\
d^{-1/2}\cdot(\log d)^{-1/2}\cdot\kappa^{-1/2}&\text{if $s^{\star}=1$}.\end{cases}

(D.33)

On the other hand, we have by
Proposition

D.4
that

\displaystyle\big|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle]\big|

\displaystyle\simeq(|\rho|\gamma)^{\ind\{s^{\star}\text{ is even}\}}\cdot(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-1-\ind\{s^{\star}\text{ is even}\}}

(D.34)

\displaystyle\geq|\rho|\gamma(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-2}

(D.35)

\displaystyle\geq d^{-(2s^{\star}-1)/4}\cdot(\log d)^{1/4},

(D.36)

where the last line holds since we focus on the neurons with
|\rho|\gtrsim d^{-1/2}
and
\gamma=d^{-1/4}(\log d)^{1/4}
.
Therefore, when
\kappa
is sufficiently large, the fluctuations are strictly bounded by half of the signal strength.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle|\langle g,\theta^{\star}\rangle|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot|\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}],\theta^{\star}\rangle|.

(D.37)

For the norm of
g
, we have by the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle\|g\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\big|\|g\|_{2}-\|{\widehat{g}}\|_{2}\big|+\big|\|{\widehat{g}}\|_{2}-\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}\big|

(D.38)

\displaystyle\qquad+\big|\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}-|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle]|\big|+\big|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle]\big|

(D.39)

\displaystyle\leq d^{-9s^{\star}}+\|{\widehat{g}}-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}+\big|\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}-|\langle{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|\big|+|\langle{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|

(D.40)

\displaystyle\lesssim\big|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{{\widehat{g}}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle]\big|+(\gamma|\rho|+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}+\sqrt{\frac{(d^{-1}\log d)^{(s^{\star}-1)/2}\cdot d\log d}{n}},

(D.41)

where in the second inequality, we use Eq. (
D.27
) and the fact that
\big|\|{\widehat{g}}\|-\big\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\big\|\big|\leq\big\|{\widehat{g}}-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\big\|
. The last inequality is deduced by combining
Proposition

D.4
and the fact that
d^{-9s^{\star}}\ll(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}
. For the leading term, it holds by
Proposition

D.4
that

\displaystyle|\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}],\theta^{\star}\rangle|\simeq\begin{cases}(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-1}&\text{if $s^{\star}$ is odd},\\
|\rho|\gamma(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-2}&\text{if $s^{\star}$ is even}.\end{cases}

(D.42)

Recall that the alignment admits the following iterative update rule:

\displaystyle|\langle\theta^{\prime},\theta^{\star}\rangle|=\left|\left\langle\frac{\theta+\eta g/\|g\|_{2}}{\|\theta+\eta g/\|g\|_{2}\|_{2}},\theta^{\star}\right\rangle\right|\geq\frac{|\langle g/\|g\|_{2},\theta^{\star}\rangle|-\eta^{-1}|\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\rangle|}{1+\eta^{-1}}.

(D.43)

In the following, let
\Delta
be the quantity defined in
Theorem

4.2
, and define

\rho^{\star}=d^{-1/4}(\log d)^{1/4}.

(D.44)

For the fixed neuron
m
, we first consider the case
s^{\star}\geq 2
and introduce two stopping times. The first one marks the end of the initial weak-alignment stage:

T_{1,m}=\min\{t\geq 0:|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|\geq\rho^{\star}\}.

(D.45)

The second one marks the first time the alignment reaches a constant level:

T_{2,m}=\min\{t\geq T_{1,m}:|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|\geq c_{\mathrm{w}}\},

(D.46)

where
c_{\mathrm{w}}>0
is a sufficiently small absolute constant to be chosen below.

Stage I: reaching
\rho^{\star}

If
|\rho_{m}^{(0)}|\geq\rho^{\star}
, then
T_{1,m}=0
and there is nothing to prove in this stage.
Otherwise,
|\rho_{m}^{(0)}|<\rho^{\star}
.
Suppose first that
s^{\star}\geq 3
is odd and
|\rho|\leq\rho^{\star}
. Combining Eq. (
D.33
), Eq. (
D.41
), and Eq. (
D.42
), we have

\displaystyle|\langle g,\theta^{\star}\rangle|

\displaystyle\gtrsim(|\rho|\cdot(d\log d)^{-1/4}+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-1},

(D.47)

\displaystyle\|g\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(|\rho|\cdot(d\log d)^{-1/4}+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-1}+d^{-(2s^{\star}-3)/4}\cdot(\log d)^{-1/4}\cdot\kappa^{-1/2}.

(D.48)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{|\langle g,\theta^{\star}\rangle|}{\|g\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{(|\rho|/\rho^{\star}+1)^{s^{\star}-1}}{(|\rho|/\rho^{\star}+1)^{s^{\star}-1}+\kappa^{-1/2}/\rho^{\star}}.

(D.49)

Since
|\rho|\leq\rho^{\star}
and
\kappa
is a sufficiently large constant, Eq. (
D.43
) implies that
|\rho^{\prime}|\geq\rho^{\star}
. Hence, for odd
s^{\star}\geq 3
, we have
T_{1,m}\leq 1
.

Now suppose that
s^{\star}
is even. Before
T_{1,m}
, the same estimates yield

\displaystyle\frac{|\langle g,\theta^{\star}\rangle|}{\|g\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{|\rho|\cdot(|\rho|/\rho^{\star}+1)^{s^{\star}-2}}{|\rho|\cdot(|\rho|/\rho^{\star}+1)^{s^{\star}-2}+\kappa^{-1/2}}.

(D.50)

Thus, by taking
\kappa
to be a sufficiently large constant, Eq. (
D.43
) gives
|\rho^{\prime}|\geq 2|\rho|
whenever
|\rho|<\rho^{\star}
. If
T_{1,m}=0
, the claim is trivial. Otherwise,

\displaystyle\rho^{\star}\geq|\rho_{m}^{(T_{1,m}-1)}|\geq 2^{T_{1,m}-1}\cdot d^{-1/2}/2,

(D.51)

and therefore
T_{1,m}\leq\frac{\log(d\log d)}{4\log 2}+2=O(\log d)
. Combining the odd and even cases,
T_{1,m}=O(\log d)
for every
s^{\star}\geq 2
.

Stage II: reaching constant alignment

We next show that
T_{2,m}-T_{1,m}=O(\log d)
. For
t\in[T_{1,m},T_{2,m}-1]
, define
r_{m}^{(t)}=|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|/\rho^{\star}
. Then
1\leq r_{m}^{(t)}<c_{\mathrm{w}}/\rho^{\star}
.
In this regime,
\gamma|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|=r_{m}^{(t)}d^{-1/2}(\log d)^{1/2}
dominates the smoothing term up to logarithmic factors. Combining Eq. (
D.33
), Eq. (
D.41
), and Eq. (
D.42
) again, and using the weaker lower bound obtained by dropping these logarithmic factors, there exists an absolute constant
C_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{|\langle{g_{m}^{(t)}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|}{\|g_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\cdot\frac{(r_{m}^{(t)})^{s^{\star}-1}}{(r_{m}^{(t)})^{s^{\star}-1}+\kappa^{-1/2}/\rho^{\star}}

(D.52)

\displaystyle\geq C_{1}\cdot\left(1\wedge\sqrt{\kappa}\,\rho^{\star}(r_{m}^{(t)})^{s^{\star}-1}\right).

(D.53)

Applying Eq. (
D.43
) and dividing by
\rho^{\star}
gives

\displaystyle r_{m}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle\geq C_{2}\cdot\left((\rho^{\star})^{-1}\wedge\sqrt{\kappa}(r_{m}^{(t)})^{s^{\star}-1}\right)-\frac{1}{3}r_{m}^{(t)}

(D.54)

for another absolute constant
C_{2}>0
, where we used
\eta>2
. Choose
\kappa
sufficiently large and then choose
c_{\mathrm{w}}>0
sufficiently small so that for all
1\leq r<c_{\mathrm{w}}/\rho^{\star}
,

C_{2}\cdot\left((\rho^{\star})^{-1}\wedge\sqrt{\kappa}r^{s^{\star}-1}\right)-\frac{1}{3}r\geq 2r.

(D.55)

It follows that
r_{m}^{(t+1)}\geq 2r_{m}^{(t)}
for all
t\in[T_{1,m},T_{2,m}-1]
. Therefore, if
T_{2,m}>T_{1,m}
,

\displaystyle 2^{T_{2,m}-T_{1,m}-1}\leq r_{m}^{(T_{2,m}-1)}<c_{\mathrm{w}}/\rho^{\star},

(D.56)

which implies
T_{2,m}-T_{1,m}\leq\lceil\log_{2}(c_{\mathrm{w}}/\rho^{\star})\rceil+1=O(\log d)
.

Strong alignment for
s^{\star}\geq 2

For all
t\geq T_{2,m}
such that
|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|\geq c_{\mathrm{w}}
, the estimates above imply

\displaystyle|\langle g_{m}^{(t)},\theta^{\star}\rangle|

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot\left(B|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|^{s^{\star}-1}-O(\Delta)\right),

(D.57)

\displaystyle\|g_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot\left(B|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|^{s^{\star}-1}+O(\Delta)\right),

(D.58)

where
B=\Omega(1)
may depend on
\rho_{m}^{(t)}
and the perturbations but is bounded away from zero on the constant-alignment region. Thus, for sufficiently large
d
,

\displaystyle A_{m}^{(t)}\defeq\frac{|\langle{g_{m}^{(t)}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|}{\|g_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}}\geq 1-C_{3}\Delta.

(D.59)

This also preserves the constant-alignment region: by Eq. (
D.43
),
|\rho_{m}^{(t+1)}|\geq c_{\mathrm{w}}
after decreasing
c_{\mathrm{w}}
if necessary.

Let
G_{m}^{(t)}=g_{m}^{(t)}/\|g_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}
. Using
A_{m}^{(t)}\geq 1-C_{3}\Delta
, the triangle inequality, and the Pythagorean theorem, we obtain

\displaystyle|\rho_{m}^{(t+1)}|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-C_{3}\Delta-\eta^{-1}|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|)^{2}}{(1-C_{3}\Delta-\eta^{-1}|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|)^{2}+(\sqrt{1-(1-C_{3}\Delta)^{2}}+\eta^{-1}\sqrt{1-(\rho_{m}^{(t)})^{2}})^{2}}

(D.60)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1-\eta^{-1})^{2}}{(1-\eta^{-1})^{2}+\eta^{-2}(1-(\rho_{m}^{(t)})^{2})}-O(\sqrt{\Delta}).

(D.61)

With
\tau=\eta^{-2}/(1-\eta^{-1})^{2}<1
, this implies

\displaystyle 1-|\rho_{m}^{(t+1)}|^{2}\leq\tau\big(1-|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|^{2}\big)+O(\sqrt{\Delta}).

(D.62)

Iterating (
D.62
), for every
\ell\geq 0
,

\displaystyle 1-|\rho_{m}^{(T_{2,m}+\ell)}|^{2}\leq\tau^{\ell}+O\big((1-\tau)^{-1}\sqrt{\Delta}\big).

(D.63)

Hence, after
\ell=O(\log(\Delta^{-1})/\log(\tau^{-1}))
additional steps, it holds that
|\rho_{m}^{(T_{2,m}+\ell)}|\geq 1-O(\sqrt{\Delta})
.

Case
s^{\star}=1

When
s^{\star}=1
, no weak-alignment phase is needed. The preceding estimates give, uniformly over the alignment level,

\displaystyle|\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}],\theta^{\star}\rangle|=B=\Omega(1),

(D.64)

and hence, using
n=\kappa d(\log d)^{2}
,

\displaystyle\frac{|\langle g_{m}^{(t)},\theta^{\star}\rangle|}{\|g_{m}^{(t)}\|_{2}}\geq 1-O((\log d)^{-1/2}\kappa^{-1/2})\geq 1-O(\Delta),

(D.65)

where now
\Delta=(\log d)^{-1/2}
. Therefore, the same strong-alignment recursion (
D.62
) applies from
t=0
, and after
O(\log(\Delta^{-1})/\log(\tau^{-1}))
steps we again obtain
|\rho_{m}^{(t)}|\geq 1-O(\sqrt{\Delta})
.

Combining the stages above, every neuron with
|\rho_{m}^{(0)}|\geq d^{-1/2}/2
reaches alignment at least
1-O(\sqrt{\Delta})
within
O(\log d+\log(\Delta^{-1})/\log(\tau^{-1}))
steps. Since there are at least
\Omega(M)
such neurons, the proof is complete.
∎

D.3
Proof of Key Results

D.3.1
Proof of
Lemma

D.3

Proof of
Lemma

D.3
.

For each
l\in[L]
, since both
\theta
and
\xi_{l}
are unit vectors and
\gamma\in(0,1/2)
,

\displaystyle\|\gamma\theta+\xi_{l}\|_{2}\geq\|\xi_{l}\|_{2}-\gamma\|\theta\|_{2}=1-\gamma\geq 1/2.

(D.66)

Hence, we can further control
\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle
as follows:

\displaystyle|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|=\frac{|\gamma\langle\theta,\theta^{\star}\rangle+\langle\xi_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|}{\|\gamma\theta+\xi_{l}\|_{2}}\leq 2(\gamma|\rho|+|\langle{\xi_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|).

(D.67)

Then on the event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
where
\max_{l\in[L]}|\langle{\xi_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|\leq\epsilon
, we conclude for all
l\in[L]
that

\displaystyle|\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq 2(\gamma|\rho|+\epsilon).

(D.68)

Next, for the second direction
v_{2}=(\theta-\rho\theta^{\star})/\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}
, we have
\langle\theta,v_{2}\rangle=\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}
.
Consequently, it follows from (
D.66
) that

\displaystyle|\langle w_{l},v_{2}\rangle|

\displaystyle=\frac{|\gamma\langle\theta,v_{2}\rangle+\langle\xi_{l},v_{2}\rangle|}{\|\gamma\theta+\xi_{l}\|_{2}}\leq 2\big(|\gamma\langle\theta,v_{2}\rangle|+|\langle\xi_{l},v_{2}\rangle|\big)\leq 2(\gamma\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}+\epsilon),

(D.69)

where the second inequality holds on the event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
.
For the remaining directions
v_{3},\ldots,v_{d}
, we again apply (
D.66
) to obtain that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle|\langle w_{l},v_{i}\rangle|

\displaystyle=\frac{|\gamma\langle\theta,v_{i}\rangle+\langle\xi_{l},v_{i}\rangle|}{\|\gamma\theta+\xi_{l}\|_{2}}\leq 2\big(|\gamma\langle\theta,v_{i}\rangle|+|\langle\xi_{l},v_{i}\rangle|\big)\leq 2\epsilon,

(D.70)

where for the second inequality we use the fact that
\langle\theta,v_{i}\rangle=0
for
i\geq 2
.

For the second part, we first note that
\theta=\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\cdot v_{2}+\rho v_{1}
.
Then on the event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle|\langle{\theta},{\xi_{l}}\rangle|\leq\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\cdot|\langle{v_{2}},{\xi_{l}}\rangle|+|\rho|\cdot|\langle{v_{1}},{\xi_{l}}\rangle|\leq 2\epsilon.

(D.71)

where the second inequality holds because
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
|\langle{v_{1}},{\xi_{l}}\rangle|\vee|\langle{v_{2}},{\xi_{l}}\rangle|\leq\epsilon
on the nice event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
.
Besides, on the nice event
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\widetilde{\epsilon})
, it holds for all
l\neq l^{\prime}
that
|\langle{\xi_{l}},{\xi_{l^{\prime}}}\rangle|\leq{\widetilde{\epsilon}}
.
Therefore, on the event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\widetilde{\epsilon})
, combining Eq. (
D.66
) and (
D.71
) yields

\displaystyle|\langle{w_{l}},{w_{l^{\prime}}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\langle{\xi_{l}},{\xi_{l^{\prime}}}\rangle|+\gamma|\langle{\xi_{l}},{\theta}\rangle|+\gamma|\langle{\xi_{l^{\prime}}},{\theta}\rangle|+\gamma^{2}\cdot\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\gamma\theta+\xi_{l}\|_{2}\cdot\|\gamma\theta+\xi_{l^{\prime}}\|_{2}}\leq 4(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon}+4\gamma\epsilon).

(D.72)

This finishes the proof.
∎

D.3.2
Proof of
Proposition

D.4

Proof of
Proposition

D.4
.

Invoking
Lemma

G.2
with the fact that
\|\theta^{\star}\|_{2}=1
, we can decompose
\langle{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle
as

\displaystyle\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}],\theta^{\star}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=s^{\star}}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)]\cdot\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s+1}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{s=s^{\star}}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{s}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s-1}(y)]\cdot\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s-1}

(D.73)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s^{\star}}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s^{\star}-1}(y)]\cdot\frac{\sqrt{s^{\star}}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}+R,

(D.74)

where the remainder term
R
is defined as

\displaystyle R:=\sum_{s=s^{\star}}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\Big({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)]\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle+{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s+1}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s}(y)]\Big)\cdot\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s}.

(D.75)

Below, we analyze the scale of each term in (
D.74
), and show that the remainder term
R
is dominated by the first term in (
D.74
) with high probability over the randomness of
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{L}
.

Analysis for the remainder term
R
in (
D.74
)

To bound
|R|
, we apply the triangle inequality with the fact that
|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|\leq 1
and get

\displaystyle|R|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=s^{\star}}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Big[|\zeta_{s}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)|+|\zeta_{s+1}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s}(y)|\Big]\cdot|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|^{s}.

(D.76)

Recall from (
2.2
) that
\zeta_{s}(y)={\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{P}}[h_{s}(x){\,|\,}y]
, so by Jensen’s inequality,
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s}(y)^{2}]\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{P}}[h_{s}(x)^{2}]=1
.
Also, by
Item

(a)
, we have
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[{\widehat{\psi}}_{s}(y)^{2}]=O(1)
.
Now applying Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we get that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[|\zeta_{s+1}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s}(y)|]

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s+1}(y)^{2}]^{1/2}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\widehat{\psi}_{s}(y)^{2}]^{1/2}=O(1),

(D.77)

and similarly for
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[|\zeta_{s}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)|]
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle|R|\lesssim\sum_{s=s^{\star}}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|^{s},

(D.78)

and it remains to bound the right hand side of the above inequality.
Recall the definition of the nice event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
in (
D.4
), where
\{v_{1}=\theta^{\star},v_{2}=(\theta-\rho\theta^{\star})/\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}},v_{3},\dots v_{d}\}
is an orthonormal basis.
Since
\gamma=o(1)
and
\epsilon=o(1)
, it follows from
Lemma

D.3
that for sufficiently large
d
,
|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|<1/2
on
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
for all
l\in[L]
.
Consequently, it holds on
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
that

\displaystyle|R|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{s=s^{\star}}^{\infty}{\sqrt{s+1}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{s-s^{\star}}\cdot\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|^{s^{\star}}\lesssim\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|^{s^{\star}}.

(D.79)

Now it remains to bound the right hand side in (
D.79
), and the strategy is to establish concentration over
\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{L}
.
To proceed, we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{w}_{l}=\begin{cases}w_{l}&\text{if }\sup_{1\leq i\leq d}|\langle\xi_{l},v_{i}\rangle|<\epsilon;\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(D.80)

Note that
\widetilde{w}_{l}=w_{l}
for any
l\in[L]
on
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
, and
\widetilde{w}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{w}_{L}
are independent random vectors.
Then by
Lemma

D.3
, it holds on
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
that

\displaystyle|\langle{{\widetilde{w}}_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|\leq 2(\gamma+\epsilon)\quad\text{for all }l\in[L].

(D.81)

Next, we consider the second moment of
|\langle{{\widetilde{w}}_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|^{s^{\star}}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{w}_{l}}[\langle\widetilde{w}_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2s^{\star}}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}\big[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2s^{\star}}\cdot\ind\big\{\sup_{i}|\langle{w_{l}},{v_{i}}\rangle|\leq\epsilon\big\}\big]\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2s^{\star}}]

(D.82)

Here we consider a different orthonormal basis given by
v_{1}^{\prime}=\theta,v_{2}^{\prime}=(\theta^{\star}-\rho\theta)/\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}},v_{3}^{\prime}=v_{3},\ldots,v_{d}^{\prime}=v_{d}
.
Under this basis,
\theta
is equivalent to
e_{1}
under the canonical basis, and
\theta^{\star}
is equivalent to
\rho e_{1}+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}e_{2}
under the canonical basis.
Then since the distribution of
\xi_{l}
is orthogonally invariant, we can apply
Lemma

G.4
to obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{w}_{l}}[\langle\widetilde{w}_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2s^{\star}}]\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2s^{\star}}]\lesssim(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{2s^{\star}}.

(D.83)

Combining the first moment bound in (
D.81
) and the second moment bound in (
D.83
), we can apply Bernstein’s inequality (
Lemma

H.10
) to
|\langle\widetilde{w}_{1},\theta^{\star}\rangle|^{s^{\star}},\ldots,|\langle\widetilde{w}_{L},\theta^{\star}\rangle|^{s^{\star}}
to obtain that there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1,1}
with
\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{1,1})\geq 1-d^{-c}/(MT)
such that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1,1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|^{s^{\star}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}|\langle\widetilde{w}_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|^{s^{\star}}

(D.84)

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(1+\sqrt{L^{-1}\log(d^{c}MT)}\Big)\cdot(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}+\frac{(\epsilon\vee\gamma)^{s^{\star}}\cdot\log(d^{c}MT)}{L}

(D.85)

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(1+\sqrt{L^{-1}\log d}\Big)\cdot(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}+\frac{(\epsilon\vee\gamma)^{s^{\star}}\cdot\log d}{L}.

(D.86)

Here we use the fact that
M\cdot T
is at most polynomial in
d
.
Moreover, by the condition (
D.8
) on
L
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L^{-1}\cdot(\epsilon\vee\gamma)^{s^{\star}}\cdot\log d&\lesssim d^{-s^{\star}/2}\leq(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}};\\
L^{-1}\cdot\log d&\lesssim 1.\end{aligned}

(D.87)

Now, combining (
D.79
), (
D.86
), and (
D.87
), we conclude that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1,1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle|R|\lesssim(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}.

(D.88)

Analysis for the dominant term in (
D.74
)

We still use
\widetilde{w}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{w}_{L}
defined in (
D.80
).
Since
|\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|\leq 1
, we can approximate the expectation
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\widetilde{w}}_{l}}[\langle{{\widetilde{w}}_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}]
as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{w}_{l}}[\langle\widetilde{w}_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}\Big[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot\ind\Big\{\sup_{i\in[d]}|\langle{w_{l}},{v_{i}}\rangle|\leq\epsilon\Big\}\Big]

(D.89)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}]\pm\Pr\Big(\sup_{i\in[d]}|\langle{w_{l}},{v_{i}}\rangle|>\epsilon\Big)

(D.90)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}]\pm\big(1-\Pr({{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)})\big),

(D.91)

where we use the fact that the event
\{\sup_{i}|\langle{w_{l}},{v_{i}}\rangle|>\epsilon\}\subset{{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)}^{c}
.
Similar to the argument for (
D.83
), it follows from
Lemma

G.4
that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}]

\displaystyle\simeq\begin{cases}\displaystyle(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-1}&\text{if $s^{\star}$ is odd};\\
\displaystyle\rho\gamma(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-2}&\text{if $s^{\star}$ is even}.\end{cases}

(D.92)

This gives rise to the first part of the main result.

Similarly, the second moment of
\langle\widetilde{w}_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\widetilde{w}_{l}}[\langle\widetilde{w}_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2(s^{\star}-1)}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}\Big[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2(s^{\star}-1)}\cdot\ind\Big\{\sup_{i}|\langle{w_{l}},{v_{i}}\rangle|\leq\epsilon\Big\}\Big]

(D.93)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{2(s^{\star}-1)}]

(D.94)

\displaystyle\simeq(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{2(s^{\star}-1)}.

(D.95)

Moreover, we have
|\langle{{\widetilde{w}}_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|\leq 2(\gamma\vee\epsilon)
on
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
.
Combining the above, applying Bernstein’s inequality (
Lemma

H.10
) to
\langle\widetilde{w}_{1},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1},\ldots,\langle\widetilde{w}_{L},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}
yields that there exists an event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1,2}
with
\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{1,2})\geq 1-d^{-c}/(MT)
such that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1,2}\cap{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\langle\widetilde{w}_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}={\mathbb{E}}_{w_{l}}[\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle^{s^{\star}-1}]+E,

(D.96)

where the error term
E
can be bounded as

\displaystyle|E|

\displaystyle\lesssim(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{L}}+\frac{(\epsilon\vee\gamma)^{s^{\star}-1}\log d}{L}+1-\Pr\big({{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)}\big)\lesssim(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}

(D.97)

where the second inequality follows from the assumption that
1-\Pr({\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon))=O(d^{-s^{\star}/2})
and the condition (
D.8
) on
L
using a similar argument as in (
D.87
).
Now, combining (
D.88
), (
D.96
), and (
D.97
) yields the second part of the main result.

Norm of
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]

Recall that
P_{\theta^{\star}}^{\perp}=I_{d}-\theta^{\star}\theta^{\star\top}
is the projection matrix onto the orthogonal complement of
\theta^{\star}
.
Then applying
Lemma

G.2
, we have

\displaystyle P_{\theta^{\star}}^{\perp}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=s^{\star}}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{s+1}}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[\zeta_{s}(y)\cdot\widehat{\psi}_{s+1}(y)]\cdot\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle^{s}\cdot P_{\theta^{\star}}^{\perp}w_{l}.

(D.98)

Since
\|P_{\theta^{\star}}^{\perp}w_{l}\|_{2}\leq 1
, recognizing that here we can apply the same argument as that for (
D.76
), we conclude that on the event
{\mathcal{E}}_{1,1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
,

\displaystyle\|P_{\theta^{\star}}^{\perp}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}\lesssim(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}.

(D.99)

Then the third part of the main result follows:

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}-|\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}],\theta^{\star}\rangle|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\|P_{\theta^{\star}}^{\perp}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}^{2}+|\langle{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|^{2}}-|\langle{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle|

(D.100)

\displaystyle\leq\|P_{\theta^{\star}}^{\perp}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\|_{2}\lesssim(|\rho|\gamma+d^{-1/2})^{s^{\star}}.

(D.101)

Finally, setting
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}={\mathcal{E}}_{1,1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{1,2}
finishes the proof.
∎

D.3.3
Proof of
Proposition

D.5

Proof of
Proposition

D.5
.

Recall from
Definition

D.2
that we consider the orthonormal basis
\{v_{1}=\theta^{\star},v_{2}=(\theta-\rho\theta^{\star})/\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}},v_{3},\ldots,v_{d}\}
.
Our goal is to show that
{\widehat{g}}
has small fluctuation along each of these directions, and we fix any
v\in\{v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}
in the sequel.

As each sample
(z_{i},y_{i})
is independently drawn from
{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}
,
\widehat{g}
is the average of
n
independent copies of
\widehat{g}_{1}
defined as

\displaystyle{\widehat{g}}_{1}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\big(\psi(y_{1},\langle w_{l},z_{1}\rangle)\cdot z_{1}-\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y_{1})\cdot w_{l}\big).

(D.102)

Thus we will analyze the moments of
\langle\widehat{g}_{1},v\rangle
, and then apply concentration inequalities to obtain the desired result.

Verifying the polynomial-like tail condition

First, we need to upper bound the
L^{r}({\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}})
-norm of
\langle{{\widehat{g}}_{1}},{v}\rangle
. To this end, we define
G_{v}:({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}})\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
as

\displaystyle G_{v}(z,y,w):=|\psi(y,\langle{w},{z}\rangle)\cdot\langle{z},{v}\rangle|+|\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)\cdot\langle{w},{v}\rangle|.

(D.103)

Also, we define the empirical measure
\mathrm{d}\mu(w)=L^{-1}\sum_{l}\delta(w_{l})
, where
\delta_{a}(\cdot)
is the point-mass assigned to
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
. It holds by the integral Minkowski’s inequality that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\langle{{\widehat{g}}_{1}},{v}\rangle|^{r}]^{1/r}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\int\mathrm{d}{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}(y,z)\Big(\int\mathrm{d}\mu(w)\cdot G_{v}(z,y,w)\Big)^{r}\Big)^{1/r}

(D.104)

\displaystyle\leq\int\mathrm{d}\mu(w)\Big(\int\mathrm{d}{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}(y,z)\cdot G_{v}(z,y,w)^{r}\Big)^{1/r}

(D.105)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}\big[\big(|\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)\cdot\langle{z},{v}\rangle|+|{\widehat{\psi}}_{1}(y)\cdot\langle{w_{l}},{v}\rangle|\big)^{r}\big]^{1/r}

(D.106)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}\big[|\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)\cdot\langle{z},{v}\rangle|^{r}\big]^{1/r}+r^{C_{p}}\cdot\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}|\langle{w_{l}},{v}\rangle|,

(D.107)

where the last inequality holds since
{\mathbb{E}}[|U+V|^{r}]^{1/r}\leq{\mathbb{E}}[|U|^{r}]^{1/r}+{\mathbb{E}}[|V|^{r}]^{1/r}
and
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|{\widehat{\psi}}_{1}(y)|^{r}]^{1/r}\lesssim r^{C_{p}}
by the polynomial-like tail condition in
4.1
.

For the second term in (
D.107
), we have on
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
that
|\langle{w_{l}},{v}\rangle|\leq 2\gamma+2\epsilon\leq 1
. Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have for the summand of the first term in (
D.107
) that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)\cdot\langle{z},{v}\rangle|^{r}]^{1/r}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)|^{2r}]^{1/2r}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\langle{z},{v}\rangle|^{2r}]^{1/2r}.

(D.108)

Note that
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\langle{z},{v}\rangle|^{2r}]^{1/2r}\leq(2r-1)!!^{1/(2r)}\lesssim r^{1/2}
, so it remains to bound
{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)|^{2r}]^{1/2r}
. We can decompose
w_{l}
into components that are correlated with and independent of
y
, and it holds that

\displaystyle\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{w_{l}-\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle\theta^{\star}},{z}\rangle+\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle\langle{\theta^{\star}},{z}\rangle

(D.109)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle^{2}}\cdot x^{\prime}+\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle x

(D.110)

where
x=\langle{\theta^{\star}},{z}\rangle
is independent of
x^{\prime}=(1-\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle^{2})^{-1/2}\cdot\langle{w_{l}-\langle{w_{l}},{\theta^{\star}}\rangle\theta^{\star}},{z}\rangle
.
Both
x,x^{\prime}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.
Therefore, we consider the Gaussian noise operator
{\mathrm{U}_{\rho}}\psi(y,x)={\mathbb{E}}_{x^{\prime}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)}[\psi(y,\rho x+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}x^{\prime})]
.
Conditioning on
(x,y)
and averaging over
x^{\prime}
, we can bound the
2r
-moment of
\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)
under
{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}
as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)|^{2r}]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}}[{\mathrm{U}_{\langle\theta^{\star},w_{l}\rangle}}\big(|\psi(y,x)|^{2r}\big)]

(D.111)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\left[{\mathrm{U}_{\langle\theta^{\star},w_{l}\rangle}}\big(|\psi(y,x)|^{2r}\big)\cdot\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(x,y)}{{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)}\right]

(D.112)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\left[|\psi(y,x)|^{2r}\cdot{\mathrm{U}_{\langle\theta^{\star},w_{l}\rangle}}\left(\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(x,y)}{{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)}\right)\right]

(D.113)

\displaystyle\leq\left({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}[|\psi(y,x)|^{4r}]\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\left[\left({\mathrm{U}_{\langle\theta^{\star},w_{l}\rangle}}\Bigl(\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(x,y)}{{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)}\Bigr)\right)^{2}\right]\right)^{1/2},

(D.114)

where the second line follows from the property of the Gaussian noise operator in (
B.7
).
By assumption of the tail bound in
4.1
, we have that

{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[|\psi(y,x)|^{4r}]\leq C_{p}(4r)^{4C_{p}r}
. For the second term, we have by spectral decomposition of Gaussian noise operator in (
B.6
) and Parseval’s identity that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\left[\left({\mathrm{U}_{\langle\theta^{\star},w_{l}\rangle}}\Bigl(\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(x,y)}{{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)}\Bigr)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{s\geq s^{\star}}\langle\theta^{\star},w_{l}\rangle^{2s}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\left[\zeta_{s}(y)^{2}\right]

(D.115)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{s\geq s^{\star}}|\langle{\theta^{\star}},{w_{l}}\rangle|^{2s}\leq 3,

(D.116)

where we use the property that on the nice event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
we have
|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|\leq 2(\gamma|\rho|+\epsilon)\leq 2(\gamma+\epsilon)<1/2
and also
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\zeta_{s}(y)^{2}]\leq 1
. In conclusion, we get for the first term in (
D.107
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}\sum_{l}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)\cdot\langle{z},{v}\rangle|^{r}]^{1/r}\lesssim r^{C_{p}+1/2}.

(D.117)

Combining everything, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[|\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle|^{r}]^{1/r}

\displaystyle\lesssim r^{C_{p}+1/2},\quad\forall v\in\{\theta^{\star},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}.

(D.118)

Calculating the second moment

To bound the variance, we have for each
v
among the designated directions that

\displaystyle\Var_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle^{2}]-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle]^{2}

(D.119)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle^{2}].

(D.120)

From this we see that it suffices to bound the second moment of
\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle
, which is given by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l,l^{\prime}=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}\left[\psi(y,\langle w_{l},z\rangle)\psi(y,\langle w_{l^{\prime}},z\rangle)\langle z,v\rangle^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l,l^{\prime}=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}\left[\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)^{2}\langle w_{l},v\rangle\langle w_{l^{\prime}},v\rangle\right]

(D.121)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l\neq l^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\bigg[\psi(y,\langle w_{l},z\rangle)\psi(y,\langle w_{l^{\prime}},z\rangle)\langle z,v\rangle^{2}\cdot\bigg(1+\sum_{s\geq s^{\star}}\zeta_{s}(y)h_{s}(\langle\theta^{\star},z\rangle)\bigg)\bigg]

(D.122)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}\left[\psi(y,\langle w_{l},z\rangle)^{2}\cdot\langle z,v\rangle^{2}\right]+\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l\neq l^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)^{2}]\langle w_{l},v\rangle\langle w_{l^{\prime}},v\rangle

(D.123)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)^{2}]\langle w_{l},v\rangle^{2}.

(D.124)

As
\psi(y,z)z
is quadruple-integrable by
4.1
, the above integral is well-defined.
We split the summation into two parts:
l=l^{\prime}
and
l\neq l^{\prime}
.
For
l=l^{\prime}
, we have for the gradient term that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\langle{z},{v}\rangle^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)^{4}]^{1/2}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{z},{v}\rangle^{4}]^{1/2}

(D.125)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{L^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)^{4}]^{1/2},

(D.126)

where the first line holds by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the second line holds since
\langle{z},{v}\rangle
is a standard normal random variable. Invoking Eq. (
D.114
) and Eq. (
D.116
), we see that each summand in the expression above is bounded by a constant. Hence

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)^{2}\cdot\langle{z},{v}\rangle^{2}]\lesssim L^{-1}.

(D.127)

For the debiasing term, it holds that
{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[{\widehat{\psi}}_{1}(y)^{2}]\leq 1
and
|\langle{w_{l}},{v}\rangle|\leq 1
, and similarly it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{L}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)^{2}]\langle w_{l},v\rangle^{2}\lesssim L^{-1}.

(D.128)

For
l\neq l^{\prime}
,
Lemma

D.3
implies that we have on the nice event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\widetilde{\epsilon})
that

\displaystyle|\langle w_{l},w_{l^{\prime}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq 4(\gamma^{2}+2\gamma\epsilon+\widetilde{\epsilon})

(D.129)

\displaystyle\leq 4(2\gamma^{2}+\epsilon^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})

(D.130)

\displaystyle\leq 8(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})\defeq\epsilon_{2},

(D.131)

where we have used the assumption that
\epsilon^{2}\leq\widetilde{\epsilon}
.
Defining

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}\defeq\max\{|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|,|\langle w_{l^{\prime}},\theta^{\star}\rangle|\},\qquad\epsilon_{0}\defeq\max\{|\langle w_{l},v\rangle|,|\langle w_{l^{\prime}},v\rangle|\},

(D.132)

we have by
Lemma

G.3
that, for any
v\in\{\theta^{\star},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}
, it holds on the same event that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\bigg[\psi(y,\langle w_{l},z\rangle)\psi(y,\langle w_{l^{\prime}},z\rangle)\langle z,v\rangle^{2}\cdot\bigg(1+\sum_{s\geq s^{\star}}\zeta_{s}(y)h_{s}(\langle\theta^{\star},z\rangle)\bigg)\bigg]

(D.133)

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\,\epsilon_{2}^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot\left(1+\frac{\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{\epsilon_{2}}+\left(\frac{\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{\epsilon_{2}}\right)^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot\epsilon+\ind(v\perp\theta^{\star})\cdot\left(\frac{\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{\epsilon_{2}}\right)^{s^{\star}-2}\cdot\frac{\epsilon_{0}^{2}}{\epsilon_{2}}\cdot(\epsilon_{1}^{2}+\epsilon_{1}\cdot\ind(s^{\star}\geq 4))\right).

(D.134)

If the nice event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
also holds, on which the following holds for all
l\in[L]
:

\displaystyle|\langle\xi_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|<\epsilon,\quad\max_{2\leq i\leq d}|\langle\xi_{l},v_{i}\rangle|<\epsilon,

(D.135)

then by
Lemma

D.3
, for any
i\geq 3
and
l\leq L
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\langle w_{l},\theta^{\star}\rangle|\lesssim\gamma|\rho|+\epsilon,\quad|\langle w_{l},v_{2}\rangle|\lesssim\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\gamma+\epsilon,\quad|\langle w_{l},v_{i}\rangle|\lesssim\epsilon,

(D.136)

Consequently, we can set
\epsilon_{1}\simeq\gamma|\rho|+\epsilon=o(1)
and

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}\simeq\begin{cases}\gamma|\rho|+\epsilon,&\text{if $v=\theta^{\star}$},\\
\gamma\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}+\epsilon,&\text{if $v=v_{2}$},\\
\epsilon,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(D.137)

Therefore, we can simplify the ratio
\epsilon_{1}^{2}/\epsilon_{2}
and
\epsilon_{0}^{2}/\epsilon_{2}
as

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon_{1}^{2}}{\epsilon_{2}}\simeq\frac{(\gamma|\rho|+\epsilon)^{2}}{4(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})}\simeq\frac{\gamma^{2}|\rho|^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon}},\quad\frac{\epsilon_{0}^{2}}{\epsilon_{2}}\simeq\begin{cases}\frac{(\gamma|\rho|+\epsilon)^{2}}{8(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})}\simeq\frac{\gamma^{2}|\rho|^{2}+\epsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon}},&\text{if $v=\theta^{\star}$},\\
\frac{(\gamma\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}+\epsilon)^{2}}{8(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})}\simeq\frac{\gamma^{2}(1-\rho^{2})+\epsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon}},&\text{if $v=v_{2}$},\\
\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})}\simeq\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon}},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(D.138)

Since
\epsilon^{2}\leq{\widetilde{\epsilon}}
, we can conclude that
\epsilon_{1}^{2}/\epsilon_{2}\lesssim 1
and
\epsilon_{0}^{2}/\epsilon_{2}\lesssim 1
. Hence, the right-hand side of (
D.134
) is bounded by
\epsilon_{2}^{s^{\star}-1}\simeq(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}
for all
v\in\{\theta^{\star},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}
.
Similarly, let us consider the term
L^{-2}\cdot\sum_{l\neq l^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)^{2}]\langle w_{l},v\rangle\langle w_{l^{\prime}},v\rangle
. On the nice event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L^{2}}\cdot\sum_{l\neq l^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}[\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)^{2}]\langle w_{l},v\rangle\langle w_{l^{\prime}},v\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon_{0}^{2}\cdot\ind\{s^{\star}\leq 2\}

(D.139)

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon_{2}\ind(s^{\star}\leq 2)

(D.140)

\displaystyle\lesssim(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}\cdot\ind\{s^{\star}\leq 2\}.

(D.141)

The first inequality holds because
\widehat{\psi}_{1}(y)=0
whenever
s^{\star}\geq 2
because of
(b)
, and the second inequality holds due to the condition that
\epsilon^{2}\leq\widetilde{\epsilon}
.

Combining the results for
l=l^{\prime}
and
l\neq l^{\prime}
, it holds for any
v\in\{\theta^{\star},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}
on the event
{\mathcal{E}}(\epsilon)\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}({\widetilde{\epsilon}})
that

\displaystyle\Var_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle]{\lesssim}(\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+\frac{1}{L}.

(D.142)

Concentration

So far we have shown that
\psi(y,\langle{w_{l}},{z}\rangle)
satisfies the condition of
Lemma

H.12
.
Additionally, we now have an upper bound on the variance of
\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle
for any
v\in\{\theta^{\star},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}
in hand.
Thus, by
Lemma

H.12
there exists a
\{(z_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in[n]}
-measurable event
{\mathcal{E}}_{2,1}
with
\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{2,1})\geq 1-d^{-c}/(MT)
, and it holds on
{\mathcal{E}}_{2,1}
that

\displaystyle\left|\langle{\widehat{g}},v\rangle-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}},v\rangle]\right|

(D.143)

\displaystyle\qquad{\lesssim}\sqrt{\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle^{2}]\cdot\log(d^{c}MT)}{n}}+\frac{\log(d^{c}MT)\cdot\log(d^{c}MTn)^{C_{p}+1/2}}{n}

(D.144)

\displaystyle\qquad{\lesssim}\sqrt{\frac{((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1})\cdot\log(d)}{n}}+\frac{\log(d)^{C_{p}+3/2}}{n},\quad\forall v\in\{\theta^{\star},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}.

(D.145)

where we use the fact that
T
,
M
, and
n
all depend polynomially on
d
. Moreover, since we assume that

\displaystyle n=\Omega\Big(\big((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1}\big)^{-1}\cdot\log(d)^{2C_{p}+2}\Big),

(D.146)

we have that the first term in (
D.145
) dominates, which further implies that

\displaystyle\left|\langle{\widehat{g}},\theta^{\star}\rangle-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\langle{\widehat{g}},\theta^{\star}\rangle]\right|\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1})\cdot\log(d)}{n}}.

(D.147)

Meanwhile, for the
\ell_{2}
-norm of
{\widehat{g}}
, we have by Jensen’s inequality that for any
r\geq 1
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[\|{\widehat{g}}_{1}\|_{2}^{r}]^{1/r}

\displaystyle=\bigg({\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}\bigg[\sum_{v\in\{\theta^{\star},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}}\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle^{2}\bigg]^{r/2}\bigg)^{1/r}

(D.148)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d}\cdot\bigg(\frac{1}{d}\cdot\sum_{v\in\{\theta^{\star},v_{2},\ldots,v_{d}\}}{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}\left[|\langle{\widehat{g}}_{1},v\rangle|^{r}\right]\bigg)^{1/r}\lesssim\sqrt{d}\cdot(r)^{C_{p}+1/2}.

(D.149)

This polynomial tail bound enables us to apply
Lemma

H.12
for the
\ell_{2}
-norm of
{\widehat{g}}
, which implies that there exists some event
{\mathcal{E}}_{2,2}
with
\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{2,2})\geq 1-d^{-c}/(MT)
, and it holds on
{\mathcal{E}}_{2,2}
that

\displaystyle\big\|{\widehat{g}}-{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{\theta^{\star}}}[{\widehat{g}}]\big\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\big((\gamma^{2}+\widetilde{\epsilon})^{s^{\star}-1}+L^{-1}\big)\cdot d\cdot\log(d)}{n}}.

(D.150)

Setting
{\mathcal{E}}_{2}={\mathcal{E}}_{2,1}\cap{\mathcal{E}}_{2,2}
gives the desired event. This concludes the proof of
Proposition

D.5
.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of the Main Theorem for the Sparse Prior

E.1
Proof Outline and Preliminaries

In this section, we provide a detailed proof for
Theorem

5.1
. We begin with some good events that we will work with.

Signal concentration

We begin with a good event on which the signal spreads almost evenly within its support. Define a series of events:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{0,r}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\|\theta^{\star}\|_{r}^{r}\leq C_{r}\cdot k^{1-r/2}\};

(E.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{0,\infty}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{\|\theta^{\star}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\infty}\cdot k^{-1/2}\log(k)^{1/2}\};

(E.2)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{0,\sharp}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Big\{\sum_{j\in[d]}\ind\big\{|\theta_{j}^{\star}|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2k}}\big\}\geq\frac{k}{4}\Big\}.

(E.3)

The following lemma guarantees that all the events above hold with high probability.

Lemma E.1

(Good signal)
.

Suppose that
k
is sufficiently large such that
k/\log(k)\geq 32c(r\vee 1)
,
k/\log^{r+2}k\geq\sqrt{2c+2}
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{0,r})\wedge\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{0,\infty})

\displaystyle\geq 1-O\big(k^{-c_{0,1}}\big);

(E.4)

\displaystyle\Pr({\mathcal{E}}_{0,\sharp})
